His flushed face, the halting speech of the usually loquacious rangeman, the subterfuge employed to &quot;sneak off,&quot;--as he himself would characterize his hasty departure,--all seem very peculiar. What could he have meant by &quot;trying&quot; me? But before I have time to evolve a satisfactory explanation, I hear Wingie tiptoeing back.

&quot;It&#39;s all right, Aleck. They won&#39;t come from the chapel for a good while yet.&quot;

&quot;What did you mean by &#39;trying&#39; me, Wingie?&quot;

&quot;Oh, well,&quot; he stammers, &quot;never min&#39;, Aleck. You are a good boy, all right. You don&#39;t belong here, that&#39;s what <em>I</em> say.&quot;

&quot;Well, I <em>am</em> here; and the chances are I&#39;ll die here.&quot;

&quot;Now, don&#39;t talk so foolish, boy. I &#39;lowed you looked down at the mouth. Now, don&#39;t you fill your head with such stuff an&#39; nonsense. Croak here, hell! You ain&#39;t goin&#39; t&#39;do nothin&#39; of the kind. Don&#39;t you go broodin&#39;, now. You listen t&#39;me, Aleck, that&#39;s your friend talkin&#39;, see? You&#39;re so young, why, you&#39;re just a kid. Twenty-one, ain&#39;t you? An&#39; talkin&#39; about dyin&#39;! Shame on you, shame!&quot;

His manner is angry, but the tremor in his voice sends a ray of warmth to my heart. Impulsively I put my hand between the bars. His firm clasp assures me of returned appreciation.

&quot;You must brace up, Aleck. Look at the lifers. You&#39;d think they&#39;d be black as night. Nit, my boy, the jolliest lot in th&#39; dump. You seen old Henry? No? Well, you ought&#39; see &#39;im. He&#39;s the oldest man here; in fifteen years. A lifer, an&#39; hasn&#39;t a friend in th&#39; woild, but he&#39;s happy as th&#39; day&#39;s long. An&#39; you got plenty friends; true blue, too. I know you have.&quot;

&quot;I have, Wingie. But what could they do for me?&quot;

&quot;How you talk, Aleck. Could do anythin&#39;. You got rich friends, I know. You was mixed up with Frick. Well, your friends are all right, ain&#39;t they?&quot;

&quot;Of course. What could they do, Wingie?&quot;

&quot;Get you pard&#39;n, in two, three years may be, see? You must make a good record here.&quot;

&quot;Oh, I don&#39;t care for a pardon.&quot;

&quot;Wha-a-t? You&#39;re kiddin&#39;.&quot;

&quot;No, Wingie, quite seriously. I am opposed to it on principle.&quot;

&quot;You&#39;re sure bugs. What you talkin&#39; &#39;bout? Principle fiddlesticks. Want to get out o&#39; here?&quot;

&quot;Of course I do.&quot;

&quot;Well, then, quit your principle racket. What&#39;s principle got t&#39; do with &#39;t? Your principle&#39;s &#39;gainst get-tin&#39; out?&quot;

&quot;No, but against being pardoned.&quot;

&quot;You&#39;re beyond me, Aleck. Guess you&#39;re joshin&#39; me.&quot;

&quot;Now listen, Wingie. You see, I wouldn&#39;t apply for a pardon, because it would be asking favors from the government, and I am against it, you understand? It would be of no use, anyhow, Wingie.&quot;

&quot;An&#39; if you could get a pard&#39;n for the askin&#39;, you won&#39;t ask, Aleck. That&#39;s what you mean?&quot;

&quot;Yes.&quot;

&quot;You&#39;re hot stuff, Aleck. What they call you, Narchist? Hot stuff, by gosh! Can&#39;t make you out, though. Seems daffy. Lis&#39;n t&#39; me, Aleck. If I was you, I&#39;d take anythin&#39; I could get, an&#39; then tell &#39;em to go t&#39;hell. That&#39;s what <em>I</em> would do, my boy.&quot;

He looks at me quizzically, searchingly. The faint echo of the Captain&#39;s step reaches us from a gallery on the opposite side. With a quick glance to right and left, Wingie leans over toward the door. His mouth between the bars, he whispers very low:

&quot;Principles opposed to a get-a-way, Aleck?&quot;

The sudden question bewilders me. The instinct of liberty, my revolutionary spirit, the misery of my existence, all flame into being, rousing a wild, tumultuous beating of my heart, pervading my whole being with hope, intense to the point of pain. I remain silent. Is it safe to trust him? He seems kind and sympathetic--

&quot;You may trust me, Aleck,&quot; Wingie whispers, as if reading my thoughts. &quot;I&#39;m your friend.&quot;

&quot;Yes, Wingie, I believe you. My principles are not opposed to an escape. I have been thinking about it, but so far--&quot;

&quot;S-sh! Easy. Walls have ears.&quot;

&quot;Any chance here, Wingie?&quot;

&quot;Well, it&#39;s a damn tough dump, this &#39;ere is; but there&#39;s many a star in heaven, Aleck, an&#39; you may have a lucky one. Hasn&#39;t been a get-a-way here since Paddy McGraw sneaked over th&#39; roof, that&#39;s--lemme see, six, seven years ago, &#39;bout.&quot;

&quot;How did he do it?&quot; I ask, breathlessly.

&quot;Jest Irish luck. They was finishin&#39; the new block, you know. Paddy was helpin&#39; lay th&#39; roof. When he got good an&#39; ready, he jest goes to work and slides down th&#39; roof. Swiped stuff in the mat shop an&#39; spliced a rope together, see. They never got &#39;im, either.&quot;

&quot;Was he in stripes, Wingie?&quot;

&quot;Sure he was. Only been in a few months.&quot;

&quot;How did he manage to get away in stripes? Wouldn&#39;t he be recognized as an escaped prisoner?&quot;

&quot;<em>That</em> bother you, Aleck? Why, it&#39;s easy. Get planted till dark, then hold up th&#39; first bloke you see an&#39; take &#39;is duds. Or you push in th&#39; back door of a rag joint; plenty of &#39;em in Allegheny.&quot;

&quot;Is there any chance now through the roof?&quot;

&quot;Nit, my boy. Nothin&#39; doin&#39; <em>there</em>. But a feller&#39;s got to be alive. Many ways to kill a cat, you know. Remember the stiff<sup><a href="#fn-25" id="ref-25" aria-label="Footnote 25">[25]</a></sup> you got in them things, tow&#39;l an&#39; soap?&quot;

&quot;You know about it, Wingie?&quot; I ask, in amazement.

&quot;Do I? He, he, you little--&quot;

The click of steel sounds warning. Wingie disappears.

CHAPTER VIII

TO THE GIRL

Direct to Box A 7, Allegheny City, Pa., November 18, 1892.

My dear Sonya:

It seems an age since I wrote to you, yet it is only a month. But the monotony of my life weights down the heels of time,--the only break in the terrible sameness is afforded me by your dear, affectionate letters, and those of Fedya. When I return to the cell for the noon meal, my step is quickened by the eager expectation of finding mail from you. About eleven in the morning, the Chaplain makes his rounds; his practiced hand shoots the letter between the bars, toward the bed or on to the little table in the corner. But if the missive is light, it will flutter to the floor. As I reach the cell, the position of the little white object at once apprises me whether the letter is long or short. With closed eyes I sense its weight, like the warm pressure of your own dear hand, the touch reaching softly to my heart, till I feel myself lifted across the chasm into your presence. The bars fade, the walls disappear, and the air grows sweet with the aroma of fresh air and flowers,--I am again with you, walking in the bright July moonlight.... The touch of the <em>velikorussian</em> in your eyes and hair conjures up the Volga, our beautiful <em>bogatir</em>,<sup><a href="#fn-26" id="ref-26" aria-label="Footnote 26">[26]</a></sup> and the strains of the <em>dubinushka</em>,<sup><a href="#fn-27" id="ref-27" aria-label="Footnote 27">[27]</a></sup> trembling with suffering and yearning, float about me.... The meal remains untouched. I dream over your letter, and again I read it, slowly, slowly, lest I reach the end too quickly. The afternoon hours are hallowed by your touch and your presence, and I am conscious only of the longing for my cell,--in the quiet of the evening, freed from the nightmare of the immediate, I walk in the garden of our dreams.

And the following morning, at work in the shop, I pass in anxious wonder whether some cheering word from my own, my real world, is awaiting me in the cell. With a glow of emotion I think of the Chaplain: perhaps at the very moment your letter is in his hands. He is opening it, reading. Why should strange eyes ... but the Chaplain seems kind and discreet. Now he is passing along the galleries, distributing the mail. The bundle grows meagre as the postman reaches the ground floor. Oh! if he does not come to my cell quickly, he may have no letters left. But the next moment I smile at the childish thought,--if there is a letter for me, no other prisoner will get it. Yet some error might happen.... No, it is impossible--my name and prison number, and the cell number marked by the Chaplain across the envelope, all insure the mail against any mistake in delivery. Now the dinner whistle blows. Eagerly I hasten to the cell. There is nothing on the floor! Perhaps on the bed, on the table.... I grow feverish with the dread of disappointment. Possibly the letter fell under the bed, or in that dark corner. No, none there,--but it can&#39;t be that there is no mail for me to-day! I must look again--it may have dropped among the blankets.... No, there is no letter!

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

Thus pass my days, dear friend. In thought I am ever with you and Fedya, in our old haunts and surroundings. I shall never get used to this life, nor find an interest in the reality of the moment. What will become of me, I don&#39;t know. I hardly care. We are revolutionists, dear: whatever sacrifices the Cause demands, though the individual perish, humanity will profit in the end. In that consciousness we must find our solace.

ALEX.

<em>Sub rosa</em>, Last Day of November, 1892.

Beloved Girl:

I thought I would not survive the agony of our meeting, but human capacity for suffering seems boundless. All my thoughts, all my yearnings, were centered in the one desire to see you, to look into your eyes, and there read the beautiful promise that has filled my days with strength and hope.... An embrace, a lingering kiss, and the gift of Lingg<sup><a href="#fn-28" id="ref-28" aria-label="Footnote 28">[28]</a></sup> would have been mine. To grasp your hand, to look down for a mute, immortal instant into your soul, and then die at your hands, Beloved, with the warm breath of your caress wafting me into peaceful eternity--oh, it were bliss supreme, the realization of our day dreams, when, in transports of ecstasy, we kissed the image of the Social Revolution. Do you remember that glorious face, so strong and tender, on the wall of our little Houston Street hallroom? How far, far in the past are those inspired moments! But they have filled my hours with hallowed thoughts, with exulting expectations. And then you came. A glance at your face, and I knew my doom to terrible life. I read it in the evil look of the guard. It was the Deputy himself. Perhaps you had been searched! He followed our every moment, like a famished cat that feigns indifference, yet is alert with every nerve to spring upon the victim. Oh, I know the calculated viciousness beneath that meek exterior. The accelerated movement of his drumming fingers, as he deliberately seated himself between us, warned me of the beast, hungry for prey.... The halo was dissipated. The words froze within me, and I could meet you only with a vapid smile, and on the instant it was mirrored in my soul as a leer, and I was filled with anger and resentment at everything about us--myself, the Deputy (I could have throttled him to death), and--at you, dear. Yes, Sonya, even at you: the quick come to bury the dead.... But the next moment, the unworthy throb of my agonized soul was stilled by the passionate pressure of my lips upon your hand. How it trembled! I held it between my own, and then, as I lifted my face to yours, the expression I beheld seemed to bereave me of my own self: it was you who were I! The drawn face, the look of horror, your whole being the cry of torture--were <em>you</em> not the real prisoner? Or was it my visioned suffering that cemented the spiritual bond, annihilating all misunderstanding, all resentment, and lifting us above time and place in the afflatus of martyrdom?

Mutely I held your hand. There was no need for words. Only the prying eyes of the catlike presence disturbed the sacred moment. Then we spoke--mechanically, trivialities.... What though the cadaverous Deputy with brutal gaze timed the seconds, and forbade the sound of our dear Russian,--nor heaven nor earth could violate the sacrament sealed with our pain.

The echo accompanied my step as I passed through the rotunda on my way to the cell. All was quiet in the block. No whir of loom reached me from the shops. Thanksgiving Day: all activities were suspended. I felt at peace in the silence. But when the door was locked, and I found myself alone, all alone within the walls of the tomb, the full significance of your departure suddenly dawned on me. The quick had left the dead.... Terror of the reality seized me and I was swept by a paroxysm of anguish--

I must close. The friend who promised to have this letter mailed <em>sub rosa</em> is at the door. He is a kind unfortunate who has befriended me. May this letter reach you safely. In token of which, send me postal of indifferent contents, casually mentioning the arrival of news from my brother in Moscow. Remember to sign &quot;Sister.&quot;

With a passionate embrace,

YOUR SASHA.

CHAPTER IX

PERSECUTION

I

Suffering and ever-present danger are quick teachers. In the three months of penitentiary life I have learned many things. I doubt whether the vague terrors pictured by my inexperience were more dreadful than the actuality of prison existence.

In one respect, especially, the reality is a source of bitterness and constant irritation. Notwithstanding all its terrors, perhaps because of them, I had always thought of prison as a place where, in a measure, nature comes into its own: social distinctions are abolished, artificial barriers destroyed; no need of hiding one&#39;s thoughts and emotions; one could be his real self, shedding all hypocrisy and artifice at the prison gates. But how different is this life! It is full of deceit, sham, and pharisaism--an aggravated counterpart of the outside world. The flatterer, the backbiter, the spy,--these find here a rich soil. The ill-will of a guard portends disaster, to be averted only by truckling and flattery, and servility fawns for the reward of an easier job. The dissembling soul in stripes whines his conversion into the pleased ears of the Christian ladies, taking care he be not surprised without tract or Bible,--and presently simulated piety secures a pardon, for the angels rejoice at the sinner&#39;s return to the fold. It sickens me to witness these scenes.

The officers make the alternative quickly apparent to the new inmate: to protest against injustice is unavailing and dangerous. Yesterday I witnessed in the shop a characteristic incident--a fight between Johnny Davis and Jack Bradford, both recent arrivals and mere boys. Johnny, a manly-looking fellow, works on a knitting machine, a few feet from my table. Opposite him is Jack, whose previous experience in a reformatory has &quot;put him wise,&quot; as he expresses it. My three months&#39; stay has taught me the art of conversing by an almost imperceptible motion of the lips. In this manner I learned from Johnny that Bradford is stealing his product, causing him repeated punishment for shortage in the task. Hoping to terminate the thefts, Johnny complained to the overseer, though without accusing Jack. But the guard ignored the complaint, and continued to report the youth. Finally Johnny was sent to the dungeon. Yesterday morning he returned to work. The change in the rosy-cheeked boy was startling: pale and hollow-eyed, he walked with a weak, halting step. As he took his place at the machine, I heard him say to the officer:

&quot;Mr. Cosson, please put me somewhere else.&quot;

&quot;Why so?&quot; the guard asked.

&quot;I can&#39;t make the task here. I&#39;ll make it on another machine, please, Mr. Cosson.&quot;

&quot;Why can&#39;t you make it here?&quot;

&quot;I&#39;m missing socks.&quot;

&quot;Ho, ho, playing the old game, are you? Want to go to th&#39; hole again, eh?&quot;

&quot;I couldn&#39;t stand the hole again, Mr. Cosson, swear to God, I couldn&#39;t. But my socks&#39;s missing here.&quot;

&quot;Missing hell! Who&#39;s stealing your socks, eh? Don&#39;t come with no such bluff. Nobody can&#39;t steal your socks while I&#39;m around. You go to work now, and you&#39;d better make the task, understand?&quot;

Late in the afternoon, when the count was taken, Johnny proved eighteen pairs short. Bradford was &quot;over.&quot;

I saw Mr. Cosson approach Johnny.

&quot;Eh, thirty, machine thirty,&quot; he shouted. &quot;You won&#39;t make the task, eh? Put your coat and cap on.&quot;

Fatal words! They meant immediate report to the Deputy, and the inevitable sentence to the dungeon.

&quot;Oh, Mr. Cosson,&quot; the youth pleaded, &quot;it ain&#39;t my fault, so help me God, it isn&#39;t.&quot;

&quot;It ain&#39;t, eh? Whose fault is it; mine?&quot;

Johnny hesitated. His eyes sought the ground, then wandered toward Bradford, who studiously avoided the look.

&quot;I can&#39;t squeal,&quot; he said, quietly.

&quot;Oh, hell! You ain&#39;t got nothin&#39; to squeal. Get your coat and cap.&quot;

Johnny passed the night in the dungeon. This morning he came up, his cheeks more sunken, his eyes more hollow. With desperate energy he worked. He toiled steadily, furiously, his gaze fastened upon the growing pile of hosiery. Occasionally he shot a glance at Bradford, who, confident of the officer&#39;s favor, met the look of hatred with a sly winking of the left eye.

Once Johnny, without pausing in the work, slightly turned his head in my direction. I smiled encouragingly, and at that same instant I saw Jack&#39;s hand slip across the table and quickly snatch a handful of Johnny&#39;s stockings. The next moment a piercing shriek threw the shop into commotion. With difficulty they tore away the infuriated boy from the prostrate Bradford. Both prisoners were taken to the Deputy for trial, with Senior Officer Cosson as the sole witness.

Impatiently I awaited the result. Through the open window I saw the overseer return. He entered the shop, a smile about the corners of his mouth. I resolved to speak to him when he passed by.

&quot;Mr. Cosson,&quot; I said, with simulated respectfulness, &quot;may I ask you a question?&quot;

&quot;Why, certainly, Burk, I won&#39;t eat you. Fire away!&quot;

&quot;What have they done with the boys?&quot;

&quot;Johnny got ten days in the hole. Pretty stiff, eh? You see, he started the fight, so he won&#39;t have to make the task. Oh, I&#39;m next to <em>him</em> all right. They can&#39;t fool me so easy, can they, Burk?&quot;

&quot;Well, I should say not, Mr. Cosson. Did you see how the fight started?&quot;

&quot;No. But Johnny admitted he struck Bradford first. That&#39;s enough, you know. &#39;Brad&#39; will be back in the shop to-morrow. I got &#39;im off easy, see; he&#39;s a good worker, always makes more than th&#39; task. He&#39;ll jest lose his supper. Guess he can stand it. Ain&#39;t much to lose, is there, Burk?&quot;

&quot;No, not much,&quot; I assented. &quot;But, Mr. Cosson, it was all Bradford&#39;s fault.&quot;

&quot;How so?&quot; the guard demanded.

&quot;He has been stealing Johnny&#39;s socks.&quot;

&quot;You didn&#39;t see him do &#39;t.&quot;

&quot;Yes, Mr. Cosson. I saw him this--&quot;

&quot;Look here, Burk. It&#39;s all right. Johnny is no good anyway; he&#39;s too fresh. You&#39;d better say nothing about it, see? My word goes with the Deputy.&quot;

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

The terrible injustice preys on my mind. Poor Johnny is already the fourth day in the dreaded dungeon. His third time, too, and yet absolutely innocent. My blood boils at the thought of the damnable treatment and the officer&#39;s perfidy. It is my duty as a revolutionist to take the part of the persecuted. Yes, I will do so. But how proceed in the matter? Complaint against Mr. Cosson would in all likelihood prove futile. And the officer, informed of my action, will make life miserable for me: his authority in the shop is absolute.

The several plans I revolve in my mind do not prove, upon closer examination, feasible. Considerations of personal interest struggle against my sense of duty. The vision of Johnny in the dungeon, his vacant machine, and Bradford&#39;s smile of triumph, keep the accusing conscience awake, till silence grows unbearable. I determine to speak to the Deputy Warden at the first opportunity.

Several days pass. Often I am assailed by doubts: is it advisable to mention the matter to the Deputy? It cannot benefit Johnny; it will involve me in trouble. But the next moment I feel ashamed of my weakness. I call to mind the much-admired hero of my youth, the celebrated Mishkin. With an overpowering sense of my own unworthiness, I review the brave deeds of Hippolyte Nikitich. What a man! Single-handed he essayed to liberate Chernishevsky from prison. Ah, the curse of poverty! But for that, Mishkin would have succeeded, and the great inspirer of the youth of Russia would have been given back to the world. I dwell on the details of the almost successful escape, Mishkin&#39;s fight with the pursuing Cossacks, his arrest, and his remarkable speech in court. Sentenced to ten years of hard labor in the Siberian mines, he defied the Russian tyrant by his funeral oration at the grave of Dmokhovsky, his boldness resulting in an additional fifteen years of <em>katorga</em>.<sup><a href="#fn-29" id="ref-29" aria-label="Footnote 29">[29]</a></sup> Minutely I follow his repeated attempts to escape, the transfer of the redoubtable prisoner to the Petropavloskaia fortress, and thence to the terrible Schluesselburg prison, where Mishkin braved death by avenging the maltreatment of his comrades on a high government official. Ah! thus acts the revolutionist; and I--yes, I am decided. No danger shall seal my lips against outrage and injustice.

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

At last an opportunity is at hand. The Deputy enters the shop. Tall and gray, slightly stooping, with head carried forward, he resembles a wolf following the trail.

&quot;Mr. McPane, one moment, please.&quot;

&quot;Yes.&quot;

&quot;I think Johnny Davis is being punished innocently.&quot;

&quot;You think, hm, hm. And who is this innocent Johnny, hm, Davis?&quot;

His fingers drum impatiently on the table; he measures me with mocking, suspicious eyes.

&quot;Machine thirty, Deputy.&quot;

&quot;Ah, yes; machine thirty; hm, hm, Reddy Davis. Hm, he had a fight.&quot;

&quot;The other man stole his stockings. I saw it, Mr. McPane.&quot;

&quot;So, so. And why, hm, hm, did you see it, my good man? You confess, then, hm, hm, you were not, hm, attending to your own work. That is bad, hm, very bad. Mr. Cosson!&quot;

The guard hastens to him.

&quot;Mr. Cosson, this man has made a, hm, hm, a charge against you. Prisoner, don&#39;t interrupt me. Hm, what is your number?&quot;

&quot;A 7.&quot;

&quot;Mr. Cosson, A 7 makes a, hm, complaint against the officer, hm, in charge of this shop. Please, hm, hm, note it down.&quot;

Both draw aside, conversing in low tones. The words &quot;kicker,&quot; &quot;his kid,&quot; reach my ears. The Deputy nods at the overseer, his steely eyes fastened on me in hatred.

II

I feel helpless, friendless. The consolation of Wingie&#39;s cheerful spirit is missing. My poor friend is in trouble. From snatches of conversation in the shop I have pieced together the story. &quot;Dutch&quot; Adams, a third-timer and the Deputy&#39;s favorite stool pigeon, had lost his month&#39;s allowance of tobacco on a prize-fight bet. He demanded that Wingie, who was stakeholder, share the spoils with him. Infuriated by refusal, &quot;Dutch&quot; reported my friend for gambling. The unexpected search of Wingie&#39;s cell discovered the tobacco, thus apparently substantiating the charge. Wingie was sent to the dungeon. But after the expiration of five days my friend failed to return to his old cell, and I soon learned that he had been ordered into solitary confinement for refusing to betray the men who had trusted him.

The fate of Wingie preys on my mind. My poor kind friend is breaking down under the effects of the dreadful sentence. This morning, chancing to pass his cell, I hailed him, but he did not respond to my greeting. Perhaps he did not hear me, I thought. Impatiently I waited for the noon return to the block. &quot;Hello, Wingie!&quot; I called. He stood at the door, intently peering between the bars. He stared at me coldly, with blank, expressionless eyes. &quot;Who are you?&quot; he whimpered, brokenly. Then he began to babble. Suddenly the terrible truth dawned on me. My poor, poor friend, the first to speak a kind word to me,--he&#39;s gone mad!

CHAPTER X

THE YEGG

I

Weeks and months pass without clarifying plans of escape. Every step, every movement, is so closely guarded, I seem to be hoping against hope. I am restive and nervous, in a constant state of excitement.

Conditions in the shop tend to aggravate my frame of mind. The task of the machine men has been increased; in consequence, I am falling behind in my work. My repeated requests for assistance have been ignored by the overseer, who improves every opportunity to insult and humiliate me. His feet wide apart, arms akimbo, belly disgustingly protruding, he measures me with narrow, fat eyes. &quot;Oh, what&#39;s the matter with you,&quot; he drawls, &quot;get a move on, won&#39;t you, Burk?&quot; Then, changing his tone, he vociferates, &quot;Don&#39;t stand there like a fool, d&#39;ye hear? Nex&#39; time I report you, to th&#39; hole you go. That&#39;s <em>me</em> talkin&#39;, understand?&quot;

Often I feel the spirit of Cain stirring within me. But for the hope of escape, I should not be able to bear this abuse and persecution. As it is, the guard is almost overstepping the limits of my endurance. His low cunning invents numerous occasions to mortify and harass me. The ceaseless dropping of the poison is making my days in the shop a constant torture. I seek relief--forgetfulness rather--in absorbing myself in the work: I bend my energies to outdo the efforts of the previous day; I compete with myself, and find melancholy pleasure in establishing and breaking high records for &quot;turning.&quot; Again, I tax my ingenuity to perfect means of communication with Johnny Davis, my young neighbor. Apparently intent upon our task, we carry on a silent conversation with eyes, fingers, and an occasional motion of the lips. To facilitate the latter method, I am cultivating the habit of tobacco chewing. The practice also affords greater opportunity for exchanging impressions with my newly-acquired assistant, an old-timer, who introduced himself as &quot;Boston Red.&quot; I owe this development to the return of the Warden from his vacation. Yesterday he visited the shop. A military-looking man, with benevolent white beard and stately carriage, he approached me, in company with the Superintendent of Prison Manufactures.

&quot;Is this the celebrated prisoner?&quot; he asked, a faint smile about the rather coarse mouth.

&quot;Yes, Captain, that&#39;s Berkman, the man who shot Frick.&quot;

&quot;I was in Naples at the time. I read about you in the English papers there, Berkman. How is his conduct, Superintendent?&quot;

&quot;Good.&quot;

&quot;Well, he should have behaved outside.&quot;

But noticing the mountain of unturned hosiery, the Warden ordered the overseer to give me help, and thus &quot;Boston Red&quot; joined me at work the next day.

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

My assistant is taking great pleasure in perfecting me in the art of lipless conversation. A large quid of tobacco inflating his left cheek, mouth slightly open and curved, he delights in recounting &quot;ghost stories,&quot; under the very eyes of the officers. &quot;Red&quot; is initiating me into the world of &quot;de road,&quot; with its free life, so full of interest and adventure, its romance, joys and sorrows. An interesting character, indeed, who facetiously pretends to &quot;look down upon the world from the sublime heights of applied cynicism.&quot;

&quot;Why, Red, you can talk good English,&quot; I admonish him. &quot;Why do you use so much slang? It&#39;s rather difficult for me to follow you.&quot;

&quot;I&#39;ll learn you, pard. See, I should have said &#39;teach&#39; you, not &#39;learn.&#39; That&#39;s how they talk in school. Have I been there? Sure, boy. Gone through college. Went through it with a bucket of coal,&quot; he amplifies, with a sly wink. He turns to expectorate, sweeping the large shop with a quick, watchful eye. Head bent over the work, he continues in low, guttural tones:

&quot;Don&#39;t care for your classic language. I can use it all right, all right. But give me the lingo, every time. You see, pard, I&#39;m no gun;<sup><a href="#fn-30" id="ref-30" aria-label="Footnote 30">[30]</a></sup> don&#39;t need it in me biz. I&#39;m a yegg.&quot;

&quot;What&#39;s a yegg, Red?&quot;

&quot;A supercilious world of cheerful idiots applies to my kind the term &#39;tramp.&#39;&quot;

&quot;A yegg, then, is a tramp. I am surprised that you should care for the life of a bum.&quot;

A flush suffuses the prison pallor of the assistant. &quot;You are stoopid as the rest of &#39;em,&quot; he retorts, with considerable heat, and I notice his lips move as in ordinary conversation. But in a moment he has regained composure, and a good-humored twinkle plays about his eyes.

&quot;Sir,&quot; he continues, with mock dignity, &quot;to say the least, you are not discriminative in your terminology. No, sir, you are not. Now, lookee here, pard, you&#39;re a good boy, but your education has been sadly neglected. Catch on? Don&#39;t call me that name again. It&#39;s offensive. It&#39;s an insult, entirely gratuitous, sir. Indeed, sir, I may say without fear of contradiction, that this insult is quite supervacaneous. Yes, sir, that&#39;s <em>me</em>. I ain&#39;t no bum, see; no such damn thing. Eliminate the disgraceful epithet from your vocabulary, sir, when you are addressing yours truly. I am a yagg, y--a--double g, sir, of the honorable clan of yaggmen. Some spell it y--e--double g, but I insist on the a, sir, as grammatically more correct, since the peerless word has no etymologic consanguinity with hen fruit, and should not be confounded by vulgar misspelling.&quot;

&quot;What&#39;s the difference between a yegg and a bum?&quot;

&quot;All the diff in the world, pard. A bum is a low-down city bloke, whose intellectual horizon, sir, revolves around the back door, with a skinny hand-out as his center of gravity. He hasn&#39;t the nerve to forsake his native heath and roam the wide world, a free and independent gentleman. That&#39;s the yagg, me bye. He dares to be and do, all bulls notwithstanding. He lives, aye, he lives,--on the world of suckers, thank you, sir. Of them &#39;tis wisely said in the good Book, &#39;They shall increase and multiply like the sands of the seashore,&#39; or words to that significant effect. A yagg&#39;s the salt of the earth, pard. A real, true-blood yagg will not deign to breathe the identical atmosphere with a city bum or gaycat. No, sirree.&quot;

I am about to ask for an explanation of the new term, when the quick, short coughs of &quot;Red&quot; warn me of danger. The guard is approaching with heavy, measured tread, head thrown back, hands clasped behind,--a sure indication of profound self-satisfaction.

&quot;How are you, Reddie?&quot; he greets the assistant.

&quot;So, so.&quot;

&quot;Ain&#39;t been out long, have you?&quot;

&quot;Two an&#39; some.&quot;

&quot;That&#39;s pretty long for you.&quot;

&quot;Oh, I dunno. I&#39;ve been out four years oncet.&quot;

&quot;Yes, you have! Been in Columbus<sup><a href="#fn-31" id="ref-31" aria-label="Footnote 31">[31]</a></sup> then, I s&#39;pose.&quot;

&quot;Not on your life, Mr. Cosson. It was Sing Sing.&quot;

&quot;Ha, ha! You&#39;re all right, Red. But you&#39;d better hustle up, fellers. I&#39;m putting in ten more machines, so look lively.&quot;

&quot;When&#39;s the machines comin&#39;, Mr. Cosson?&quot;

&quot;Pretty soon, Red.&quot;

The officer passing on, &quot;Red&quot; whispers to me:

&quot;Aleck, &#39;pretty soon&#39; is jest the time I&#39;ll quit. Damn his work and the new machines. I ain&#39;t no gaycat to work. Think I&#39;m a nigger, eh? No, sir, the world owes me a living, and I generally manage to get it, you bet you. Only mules and niggers work. I&#39;m a free man; I can live on my wits, see? I don&#39;t never work outside; damme if I&#39;ll work here. I ain&#39;t no office-seeker. What d&#39; I want to work for, eh? Can you tell me <em>that</em>?&quot;

&quot;Are you going to refuse work?&quot;

&quot;Refuse? Me? Nixie. That&#39;s a crude word, that. No, sir, I never refuse. They&#39;ll knock your damn block off, if you refuse. I merely avoid, sir, discriminately end with steadfast purpose. Work is a disease, me bye. One must exercise the utmost care to avoid contagion. It&#39;s a regular pest. <em>You</em> never worked, did you?&quot;

The unexpected turn surprises me into a smile, which I quickly suppress, however, observing the angry frown on &quot;Red&#39;s&quot; face.

&quot;You bloke,&quot; he hisses, &quot;shut your face; the screw&#39;ll pipe you. You&#39;ll get us in th&#39; hole for chewin&#39; th&#39; rag. Whatcher hehawin&#39; about?&quot; he demands, repeating the manoeuvre of pretended expectoration. &quot;D&#39;ye mean t&#39; tell me you work?&quot;

&quot;I am a printer, a compositor,&quot; I inform him.

&quot;Get off! You&#39;re an Anarchist. I read the papers, sir. You people don&#39;t believe in work. You want to divvy up. Well, it is all right, I&#39;m with you. Rockefeller has no right to the whole world. He ain&#39;t satisfied with that, either; he wants a fence around it.&quot;

&quot;The Anarchists don&#39;t want to &#39;divvy up,&#39; Red. You got your misinformation--&quot;

&quot;Oh, never min&#39;, pard. I don&#39; take stock in reforming the world. It&#39;s good enough for suckers, and as Holy Writ says, sir, &#39;Blessed be they that neither sow nor hog; all things shall be given unto them.&#39; Them&#39;s wise words, me bye. Moreover, sir, neither you nor me will live to see a change, so why should I worry me nut about &#39;t? It takes all my wits to dodge work. It&#39;s disgraceful to labor, and it keeps me industriously busy, sir, to retain my honor and self-respect. Why, you know, pard, or perhaps you don&#39;t, greenie, Columbus is a pretty tough dump; but d&#39;ye think I worked the four-spot there? Not me; no, sirree!&quot;

&quot;Didn&#39;t you tell Cosson you were in Sing Sing, not in Columbus?&quot;

&quot;&#39;Corse I did. What of it? Think I&#39;d open my guts to my Lord Bighead? I&#39;ve never been within thirty miles of the York pen. It was Hail Columbia all right, but that&#39;s between you an&#39; I, savvy. Don&#39; want th&#39; screws to get next.&quot;

&quot;Well, Red, how did you manage to keep away from work in Columbus?&quot;

&quot;Manage? That&#39;s right, sir. &#39;Tis a word of profound significance, quite adequately descriptive of my humble endeavors. Just what I did, buddy. I managed, with a capital M. To good purpose, too, me bye. Not a stroke of work in a four-spot. How? I had Billie with me, that&#39;s me kid, you know, an&#39; a fine boy he was, too. I had him put a jigger on me; kept it up for four years. There&#39;s perseverance and industry for you, sir.&quot;

&quot;What&#39;s &#39;putting a jigger on&#39;?&quot;

&quot;A jigger? Well, a jigger is--&quot;

The noon whistle interrupts the explanation. With a friendly wink in my direction, the assistant takes his place in the line. In silence we march to the cell-house, the measured footfall echoing a hollow threat in the walled quadrangle of the prison yard.

II

Conversation with &quot;Boston Red,&quot; Young Davis, and occasional other prisoners helps to while away the tedious hours at work. But in the solitude of the cell, through the long winter evenings, my mind dwells in the outside world. Friends, the movement, the growing antagonisms, the bitter controversies between the <em>Mostianer</em> and the defenders of my act, fill my thoughts and dreams. By means of fictitious, but significant, names, Russian and German words written backward, and similar devices, the Girl keeps me informed of the activities in our circles. I think admiringly, yet quite impersonally, of her strenuous militancy in championing my cause against all attacks. It is almost weak on my part, as a terrorist of Russian traditions, to consider her devotion deserving of particular commendation. She is a revolutionist; it is her duty to our common Cause. Courage, whole-souled zeal, is very rare, it is true. The Girl. Fedya, and a few others,--hence the sad lack of general opposition in the movement to Most&#39;s attitude.... But communications from comrades and unknown sympathizers germinate the hope of an approaching reaction against the campaign of denunciation. With great joy I trace the ascending revolutionary tendency in <em>Der Arme Teufel</em>. I have persuaded the Chaplain to procure the admission of the ingenious Robert Reitzel&#39;s publication. All the other periodicals addressed to me are regularly assigned to the waste basket, by orders of the Deputy. The latter refused to make an exception even in regard to the <em>Knights of Labor Journal</em>. &quot;It is an incendiary Anarchist sheet,&quot; he persisted.

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

The arrival of the <em>Teufel</em> is a great event. What joy to catch sight of the paper snugly reposing between the legs of the cell table! Tenderly I pick it up, fondling the little visitor with quickened pulse. It is an animate, living thing, a ray of warmth in the dreary evenings. What cheering message does Reitzel bring me now? What beauties of his rich mind are hidden to-day in the quaint German type? Reverently I unfold the roll. The uncut sheet opens on the fourth page, and the stirring paean of Hope&#39;s prophecy greets my eye,--

Gruss an Alexander Berkman!

For days the music of the Dawn rings in my ears. Again and again recurs the refrain of faith and proud courage,

Schon ruestet sich der freiheit Schaar Zur heiligen Entscheidungschlacht; Es enden &quot;zweiundzwanzig&quot; Jahr&#39; Vielleicht in e i n e r Sturmesnacht!

But in the evening, when I return to the cell, reality lays its heavy hand upon my heart. The flickering of the candle accentuates the gloom, and I sit brooding over the interminable succession of miserable days and evenings and nights.... The darkness gathers around the candle, as I motionlessly watch its desperate struggle to be. Its dying agony, ineffectual and vain, presages my own doom, approaching, inevitable. Weaker and fainter grows the light, feebler, feebler--a last spasm, and all is utter blackness.

Three bells. &quot;Lights out!&quot;

Alas, mine did not last its permitted hour....

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

The sun streaming into the many-windowed shop routs the night, and dispels the haze of the fire-spitting city. Perhaps my little candle with its bold defiance has shortened the reign of darkness,--who knows? Perhaps the brave, uneven struggle coaxed the sun out of his slumbers, and hastened the coming of Day. The fancy lures me with its warming embrace, when suddenly the assistant startles me:

&quot;Say, pard, slept bad last night? You look boozy, me lad.&quot;

Surprised at my silence, he admonishes me:

&quot;Young man, keep a stiff upper lip. Just look at me! Permit me to introduce to you, sir, a gentleman who has sounded the sharps and flats of life, and faced the most intricate network, sir, of iron bars between York and Frisco. Always acquitted himself with flying colors, sir, merely by being wise and preserving a stiff upper lip; see th&#39; point?&quot;

&quot;What are you driving at, Red?&quot;

&quot;They&#39;se goin&#39; to move me down on your row,<sup><a href="#fn-32" id="ref-32" aria-label="Footnote 32">[32]</a></sup> now that I&#39;m in this &#39;ere shop. Dunno how long I shall choose to remain, sir, in this magnificent hosiery establishment, but I see there&#39;s a vacant cell next yours, an&#39; I&#39;m goin&#39; to try an&#39; land there. Are you next, me bye? I&#39;m goin&#39; to learn you to be wise, sonny. I shall, so to speak, assume benevolent guardianship over you; over you and your morals, yes, sir, for you&#39;re my kid now, see?&quot;

&quot;How, your kid?&quot;

&quot;How? My kid, of course. That&#39;s just what I mean. Any objections, sir, as the learned gentlemen of the law say in the honorable courts of the blind goddess. You betcher life she&#39;s blind, blind as an owl on a sunny midsummer day. Not in your damn smoky city, though; sun&#39;s ashamed here. But &#39;way down in my Kentucky home, down by the Suanee River, Sua-a-nee-ee Riv--&quot;

&quot;Hold on, Red. You are romancing. You started to tell me about being your &#39;kid&#39;. Now explain, what do you mean by it?&quot;

&quot;Really, you--&quot; He holds the unturned stocking suspended over the post, gazing at me with half-closed, cynical eyes, in which doubt struggles with wonder. In his astonishment he has forgotten his wonted caution, and I warn him of the officer&#39;s watchful eye.

&quot;Really, Alex; well, now, damme, I&#39;ve seen something of this &#39;ere round globe, some mighty strange sights, too, and there ain&#39;t many things to surprise me, lemme tell you. But <em>you</em> do, Alex; yes, me lad, you do. Haven&#39;t had such a stunnin&#39; blow since I first met Cigarette Jimmie in Oil City. Innocent? Well, I should snicker. He was, for sure. Never heard a ghost story; was fourteen, too. Well, I got &#39;im all right, ah right. Now he&#39;s doin&#39; a five-bit down in Kansas, poor kiddie. Well, he certainly was a surprise. But many tempestuous billows of life, sir, have since flown into the shoreless ocean of time, yes, sir, they have, but I never got such a stunner as you just gave me. Why, man, it&#39;s a body-blow, a reg&#39;lar knockout to my knowledge of the world, sir, to my settled estimate of the world&#39;s supercilious righteousness. Well, damme, if I&#39;d ever believe it. Say, how old are you, Alex?&quot;

&quot;I&#39;m over twenty-two, Red. But what has all this to do with the question I asked you?&quot;

&quot;Everythin&#39;, me bye, everythin&#39;. You&#39;re twenty-two and don&#39;t know what a kid is! Well, if it don&#39;t beat raw eggs, I don&#39;t know what does. Green? Well, sir, it would be hard to find an adequate analogy to your inconsistent immaturity of mind; aye, sir, I may well say, of soul, except to compare it with the virtuous condition of green corn in the early summer moon. You know what &#39;moon&#39; is, don&#39;t you?&quot; he asks, abruptly, with an evident effort to suppress a smile.

I am growing impatient of his continuous avoidance of a direct answer. Yet I cannot find it in my heart to be angry with him; the face expressive of a deep-felt conviction of universal wisdom, the eyes of humorous cynicism, and the ludicrous manner of mixing tramp slang with &quot;classic&quot; English, all disarm my irritation. Besides, his droll chatter helps to while away the tedious hours at work; perhaps I may also glean from this experienced old-timer some useful information regarding my plans of escape.

&quot;Well, d&#39;ye know a moon when you see &#39;t?&quot; &quot;Red&quot; inquires, chaffingly.

&quot;I suppose I do.&quot;

&quot;I&#39;ll bet you my corn dodger you don&#39;t. Sir, I can see by the tip of your olfactory organ that you are steeped in the slough of densest ignorance concerning the supreme science of moonology. Yes, sir, do not contradict me. I brook no sceptical attitude regarding my undoubted and proven perspicacity of human nature. How&#39;s that for classic style, eh? That&#39;ll hold you down a moment, kid. As I was about to say when you interrupted--eh, what? You didn&#39;t? Oh, what&#39;s the matter with you? Don&#39;t yer go now an&#39; rooin the elegant flight of my rhetorical Pegasus with an insignificant interpolation of mere fact. None of your lip, now, boy, an&#39; lemme develop this sublime science of moonology before your wondering gaze. To begin with, sir, moonology is an exclusively aristocratic science. Not for the pretenders of Broad Street and Fifth Avenue. Nixie. But for the only genuine aristocracy of de road, sir, for the pink of humankind, for the yaggman, me lad, for yours truly and his clan. Yes, sirree!&quot;

&quot;I don&#39;t know what you are talking about.&quot;

&quot;I know you don&#39;t. That&#39;s why I&#39;m goin&#39; to chaperon you, kid. In plain English, sir, I shall endeavor to generate within your postliminious comprehension a discriminate conception of the subject at issue, sir, by divesting my lingo of the least shadow of imperspicuity or ambiguity. Moonology, my Marktwainian Innocent, is the truly Christian science of loving your neighbor, provided he be a nice little boy. Understand now?&quot;

&quot;How can you love a boy?&quot;

&quot;Are you really so dumb? You are not a ref boy, I can see that.&quot;

&quot;Red, if you&#39;d drop your stilted language and talk plainly, I&#39;d understand better.&quot;

&quot;Thought you liked the classic. But you ain&#39;t long on lingo neither. How can a self-respecting gentleman explain himself to you? But I&#39;ll try. You love a boy as you love the poet-sung heifer, see? Ever read Billy Shakespeare? Know the place, &#39;He&#39;s neither man nor woman; he&#39;s punk.&#39; Well, Billy knew. A punk&#39;s a boy that&#39;ll....&quot;

&quot;What!&quot;

&quot;Yes, sir. Give himself to a man. Now we&#39;se talkin&#39; plain. Savvy now, Innocent Abroad?&quot;

&quot;I don&#39;t believe what you are telling me, Red.&quot;

&quot;You don&#39;t be-lie-ve? What th&#39; devil--damn me soul t&#39; hell, what d&#39; you mean, you don&#39;t b&#39;lieve? Gee, look out!&quot;

The look of bewilderment on his face startles me. In his excitement, he had raised his voice almost to a shout, attracting the attention of the guard, who is now hastening toward us.

&quot;Who&#39;s talkin&#39; here?&quot; he demands, suspiciously eyeing the knitters. &quot;You, Davis?&quot;

&quot;No, sir.&quot;

&quot;Who was, then?&quot;

&quot;Nobody here, Mr. Cosson.&quot;

&quot;Yes, they was. I heard hollerin&#39;.&quot;

&quot;Oh, that was me,&quot; Davis replies, with a quick glance at me. &quot;I hit my elbow against the machine.&quot;

&quot;Let me see &#39;t.&quot;

The guard scrutinizes the bared arm.

&quot;Wa-a-ll,&quot; he says, doubtfully, &quot;it don&#39;t look sore.&quot;

&quot;It hurt, and I hollered.&quot;

The officer turns to my assistant: &quot;Has he been talkin&#39;, Reddie?&quot;

&quot;I don&#39;t think he was, Cap&#39;n.&quot;

Pleased with the title, Cosson smiles at &quot;Red,&quot; and passes on, with a final warning to the boy: &quot;Don&#39;t you let me catch you at it again, you hear!&quot;

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

During the rest of the day the overseers exercise particular vigilance over our end of the shop. But emboldened by the increased din of the new knitting machinery, &quot;Red&quot; soon takes up the conversation again.

&quot;Screws can&#39;t hear us now,&quot; he whispers, &quot;&#39;cept they&#39;s close to us. But watch your lips, boy; the damn bulls got sharp lamps. An&#39; don&#39; scare me again like that. Why, you talk so foolish, you make me plumb forget myself. Say, that kid is all to the good, ain&#39;t he? What&#39;s his name, Johnny Davis? Yes, a wise kid all right. Just like me own Billie I tole you &#39;bout. He was no punk, either, an&#39; don&#39;t you forget it. True as steel, he was; stuck to me through my four-spot like th&#39; bark to a tree. Say, what&#39;s that you said, you don&#39;t believe what I endeavored so conscientiously, sir, to drive into your noodle? You was only kiddin&#39; me, wasn&#39;t you?&quot;

&quot;No, Red, I meant it quite seriously. You&#39;re spinning ghost stories, or whatever you call it. I don&#39;t believe in this kid love.&quot;

&quot;An&#39; why don&#39;t you believe it?&quot;

&quot;Why--er--well, I don&#39;t think it possible.&quot;

&quot;<em>What</em> isn&#39;t possible?&quot;

&quot;You know what I mean. I don&#39;t think there can be such intimacy between those of the same sex.&quot;

&quot;Ho, ho! <em>That&#39;s</em> your point? Why, Alex, you&#39;re more of a damfool than the casual observer, sir, would be apt to postulate. You don&#39;t believe it possible, you don&#39;t, eh? Well, you jest gimme half a chance, an I&#39;ll show you.&quot;

&quot;Red, don&#39;t you talk to me like that,&quot; I burst out, angrily. &quot;If you--&quot;

&quot;Aisy, aisy, me bye,&quot; he interrupts, good-naturedly. &quot;Don&#39;t get on your high horse. No harm meant, Alex. You&#39;re a good boy, but you jest rattle me with your crazy talk. Why, you&#39;re bugs to say it&#39;s impossible. Man alive, the dump&#39;s chuckful of punks. It&#39;s done in every prison, an&#39; on th&#39; road, everywhere. Lord, if I had a plunk for every time I got th&#39; best of a kid, I&#39;d rival Rockefeller, sir; I would, me bye.&quot;

&quot;You actually confess to such terrible practices? You&#39;re disgusting. But I don&#39;t really believe it, Red.&quot;

&quot;Confess hell! I confess nothin&#39;. Terrible, disgusting! You talk like a man up a tree, you holy sky-pilot.&quot;

&quot;Are there no women on the road?&quot;

&quot;Pshaw! Who cares for a heifer when you can get a kid? Women are no good. I wouldn&#39;t look at &#39;em when I can have my prushun.<sup><a href="#fn-33" id="ref-33" aria-label="Footnote 33">[33]</a></sup> Oh, it is quite evident, sir, you have not delved into the esoteric mysteries of moonology, nor tasted the mellifluous fruit on the forbidden tree of--&quot;

&quot;Oh, quit!&quot;

&quot;Well, you&#39;ll know better before <em>your</em> time&#39;s up, me virtuous sonny.&quot;

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

For several days my assistant fails to appear in the shop on account of illness. He has been &quot;excused&quot; by the doctor, the guard informs me. I miss his help at work; the hours drag heavier for lack of &quot;Red&#39;s&quot; companionship. Yet I am gratified by his absence. His cynical attitude toward woman and sex morality has roused in me a spirit of antagonism. The panegyrics of boy-love are deeply offensive to my instincts. The very thought of the unnatural practice revolts and disgusts me. But I find solace in the reflection that &quot;Red&#39;s&quot; insinuations are pure fabrication; no credence is to be given them. Man, a reasonable being, could not fall to such depths; he could not be guilty of such unspeakably vicious practices. Even the lowest outcast must not be credited with such perversion, such depravity. I should really take the matter more calmly. The assistant is a queer fellow; he is merely teasing me. These things are not credible; indeed, I don&#39;t believe they are possible. And even if they were, no human being would be capable of such iniquity. I must not suffer &quot;Red&#39;s&quot; chaffing to disturb me.

CHAPTER XI

THE ROUTE SUB ROSA

March 4, 1893.

GIRL AND TWIN:

I am writing with despair in my heart. I was taken to Pittsburgh as a witness in the trial of Nold and Bauer. I had hoped for an opportunity--you understand, friends. It was a slender thread, but I clung to it desperately, prepared to stake everything on it. It proved a broken straw. Now I am back, and I may never leave this place alive.

I was bitterly disappointed not to find you in the courtroom. I yearned for the sight of your faces. But you were not there, nor any one else of our New York comrades. I knew what it meant: you are having a hard struggle to exist. Otherwise perhaps something could be done to establish friendly relations between Rakhmetov and Mr. Gebop.<sup><a href="#fn-34" id="ref-34" aria-label="Footnote 34">[34]</a></sup> It would require an outlay beyond the resources of our own circle; others cannot be approached in this matter. Nothing remains but the &quot;inside&quot; developments,--a terribly slow process.

This is all the hope I can hold out to you, dear friends. You will think it quite negligible; yet it is the sole ray that has again and again kindled life in moments of utmost darkness.... I did not realize the physical effects of my stay here (it is five months now) till my return from court. I suppose the excitement of being on the outside galvanized me for the nonce.... My head was awhirl; I could not collect my thoughts. The wild hope possessed me,--<em>pobeg</em>! The click of the steel, as I was handcuffed to the Deputy, struck my death-knell.... The unaccustomed noise of the streets, the people and loud voices in the courtroom, the scenes of the trial, all absorbed me in the moment. It seemed to me as if I were a spectator, interested, but personally unconcerned, in the surroundings; and these, too, were far away, of a strange world in which I had no part. Only when I found myself alone in the cell, the full significance of the lost occasion was borne in upon me with crushing force.

But why sadden you? There is perhaps a cheerier side, now that Nold and Bauer are here. I have not seen them yet, but their very presence, the circumstance that somewhere within these walls there are <em>comrades</em>, men who, like myself, suffer for an ideal--the thought holds a deep satisfaction for me. It brings me closer, in a measure, to the environment of political prisoners in Europe. Whatever the misery and torture of their daily existence, the politicals--even in Siberia--breathe the atmosphere of solidarity, of appreciation. What courage and strength there must be for them in the inspiration radiated by a common cause! Conditions here are entirely different. Both inmates and officers are at loss to &quot;class&quot; me. They have never known political prisoners. That one should sacrifice or risk his life with no apparent personal motives, is beyond their comprehension, almost beyond their belief. It is a desert of sordidness that constantly threatens to engulf one. I would gladly exchange places with our comrades in Siberia.

The former <em>podpoilnaya</em><sup><a href="#fn-35" id="ref-35" aria-label="Footnote 35">[35]</a></sup> was suspended, because of the great misfortune that befell my friend Wingie, of whom I wrote to you before. This dove will be flown by Mr. Tiuremshchick,<sup><a href="#fn-36" id="ref-36" aria-label="Footnote 36">[36]</a></sup> an old soldier who really sympathizes with Wingie. I believe they served in the same regiment. He is a kindly man, who hates his despicable work. But there is a family at home, a sick wife--you know the old, weak-kneed tale. I had a hint from him the other day: he is being spied upon; it is dangerous for him to be seen at my cell, and so forth. It is all quite true; but what he means is, that a little money would be welcome. You know how to manage the matter. Leave no traces.

I hear the felt-soled step. It&#39;s the soldier. I bid my birdie a hasty good-bye.

SASHA.

CHAPTER XII

"ZUCHTHAUSBLUETHEN"

I

A dense fog rises from the broad bosom of the Ohio. It ensnares the river banks in its mysterious embrace, veils tree and rock with sombre mist, and mocks the sun with angry frown. Within the House of Death is felt the chilling breath, and all is quiet and silent in the iron cages.

Only an occasional knocking, as on metal, disturbs the stillness. I listen intently. Nearer and more audible seem the sounds, hesitating and apparently intentional I am involuntarily reminded of the methods of communication practiced by Russian politicals, and I strive to detect some meaning in the tapping. It grows clearer as I approach the back wall of the cell, and instantly I am aware of a faint murmur in the privy. Is it fancy, or did I hear my name?

&quot;Halloa!&quot; I call into the pipe.

The knocking ceases abruptly. I hear a suppressed, hollow voice: &quot;That you, Aleck?&quot;

&quot;Yes. Who is it?&quot;

&quot;Never min&#39;. You must be deaf not to hear me callin&#39; you all this time. Take that cott&#39;n out o&#39; your ears.&quot;

&quot;I didn&#39;t know you could talk this way.&quot;

&quot;You didn&#39;t? Well, you know now. Them&#39;s empty pipes, no standin&#39; water, see? Fine t&#39; talk. Oh, dammit to--&quot;

The words are lost in the gurgle of rushing water. Presently the flow subsides, and the knocking is resumed. I bend over the privy.

&quot;Hello, hello! That you, Aleck?&quot;

&quot;Git off that line, ye jabberin&#39; idiot!&quot; some one shouts into the pipe.

&quot;Lay down, there!&quot;

&quot;Take that trap out o&#39; the hole.&quot;

&quot;Quit your foolin&#39;, Horsethief.&quot;

&quot;Hey, boys, stop that now. That&#39;s me, fellers. It&#39;s Bob, Horsethief Bob. I&#39;m talkin&#39; business. Keep quiet now, will you? Are you there, Aleck? Yes? Well, pay no &#39;tention to them dubs. &#39;Twas that crazy Southside Slim that turned th&#39; water on--&quot;

&quot;Who you call crazy, damn you,&quot; a voice interrupts.

&quot;Oh, lay down, Slim, will you? Who said you was crazy? Nay, nay, you&#39;re bugs. Hey, Aleck, you there?&quot;

&quot;Yes, Bob.&quot;

&quot;Oh, got me name, have you? Yes, I&#39;m Bob, Horsethief Bob. Make no mistake when you see me; I&#39;m Big Bob, the Horsethief. Can you hear me? It&#39;s you, Aleck?&quot;

&quot;Yes, yes.&quot;

&quot;Sure it&#39;s you? Got t&#39; tell you somethin&#39;. What&#39;s your number?&quot;

&quot;A 7.&quot;

&quot;Right you are. What cell?&quot;

&quot;6 K.&quot;

&quot;An&#39; this is me, Big Bob, in--&quot;

&quot;Windbag Bob,&quot; a heavy bass comments from above.

&quot;Shut up, Curley, I&#39;m on th&#39; line. I&#39;m in 6 F, Aleck, top tier. Call me up any time I&#39;m in, ha, ha! You see, pipe&#39;s runnin&#39; up an&#39; down, an&#39; you can talk to any range you want, but always to th&#39; same cell as you&#39;re in, Cell 6, understand? Now if you wan&#39; t&#39; talk to Cell 14, to Shorty, you know--&quot;

&quot;I don&#39;t want to talk to Shorty. I don&#39;t know him, Bob.&quot;

&quot;Yes, you do. You list&#39;n what I tell you, Aleck, an&#39; you&#39;ll be all right. That&#39;s me talkin&#39;, Big Bob, see? Now, I say if you&#39;d like t&#39; chew th&#39; rag with Shorty, you jest tell me. Tell Brother Bob, an&#39; he&#39;ll connect you all right. Are you on? Know who&#39;s Shorty?&quot;

&quot;No.&quot;

&quot;Yo oughter. That&#39;s Carl, Carl Nold. Know <em>him</em>, don&#39;t you?&quot;

&quot;What!&quot; I cry in astonishment. &quot;Is it true, Bob? Is Nold up there on your gallery?&quot;

&quot;Sure thing. Cell 14.&quot;

&quot;Why didn&#39;t you say so at once? You&#39;ve been talking ten minutes now. Did you see him?&quot;

&quot;What&#39;s your hurry, Aleck? <em>You</em> can&#39;t see &#39;im; not jest now, anyway. P&#39;r&#39;aps bimeby, mebbe. There&#39;s no hurry, Aleck. <em>You</em> got plenty o&#39; time. A few years, <em>rather</em>, ha, ha, ha!&quot;

&quot;Hey, there, Horsethief, quit that!&quot; I recognize &quot;Curley&#39;s&quot; deep bass. &quot;What do you want to make the kid feel bad for?&quot;

&quot;No harm meant, Curley,&quot; Bob returns, &quot;I was jest joshin&#39; him a bit.&quot;

&quot;Well, quit it.&quot;

&quot;You don&#39; min&#39; it, Aleck, do you?&quot; I hear Bob again, his tones softened, &quot;I didn&#39; mean t&#39; hurt your feelin&#39;s. I&#39;m your friend, Aleck, you can bet your corn dodger on that. Say, I&#39;ve got somethin&#39; for you from Shorty, I mean Carl, you savvy?&quot;

&quot;What have you, Bob?&quot;

&quot;Nixie through th&#39; hole, ain&#39;t safe. I&#39;m coffee-boy on this &#39;ere range. I&#39;ll sneak around to you in the mornin&#39;, when I go t&#39; fetch me can of bootleg. Now, jiggaroo,<sup><a href="#fn-37" id="ref-37" aria-label="Footnote 37">[37]</a></sup> screw&#39;s comin&#39;.&quot;

II

The presence of my comrades is investing existence with interest and meaning. It has brought to me a breeze from the atmosphere of my former environment; it is stirring the graves, where lie my soul&#39;s dead, into renewed life and hope.

The secret exchange of notes lends color to the routine. It is like a fresh mountain streamlet joyfully rippling through a stagnant swamp. At work in the shop, my thoughts are engrossed with our correspondence. Again and again I review the arguments elucidating to my comrades the significance of my <em>Attentat</em>: they, too, are inclined to exaggerate the importance of the purely physical result. The exchange of views gradually ripens our previously brief and superficial acquaintance into closer intimacy. There is something in Carl Nold that especially attracts me: I sense in him a congenial spirit. His spontaneous frankness appeals to me; my heart echoes his grief at the realization of Most&#39;s unpardonable behavior. But the ill-concealed antagonism of Bauer is irritating. It reflects his desperate clinging to the shattered idol. Presently, however, a better understanding begins to manifest itself. The big, jovial German has earned my respect; he braved the anger of the judge by consistently refusing to betray the man who aided him in the distribution of the Anarchist leaflet among the Homestead workers. On the other hand, both Carl and Henry appreciate my efforts on the witness stand, to exonerate them from complicity in my act. Their condemnation, as acknowledged Anarchists, was, of course, a foregone conclusion, and I am gratified to learn that neither of my comrades had entertained any illusions concerning the fate that awaited them. Indeed, both have expressed surprise that the maximum revenge of the law was not visited upon them. Their philosophical attitude exerts a soothing effect upon me. Carl even voices satisfaction that the sentence of five years will afford him a long-needed vacation from many years of ceaseless factory toil. He is facetiously anxious lest capitalist industry be handicapped by the loss of such a splendid carpenter as Henry, whom he good-naturedly chaffs on the separation from his newly affianced.

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

The evening hours have ceased to drag: there is pleasure and diversion in the correspondence. The notes have grown into bulky letters, daily cementing our friendship. We compare views, exchange impressions, and discuss prison gossip. I learn the history of the movement in the twin cities, the personnel of Anarchist circles, and collect a fund of anecdotes about Albrecht, the philosophic old shoemaker whose diminutive shop in Allegheny is the center of the radical <em>inteligenzia</em>. With deep contrition Bauer confesses how narrowly he escaped the role of my executioner. My unexpected appearance in their midst, at the height of the Homestead struggle, had waked suspicion among the Allegheny comrades. They sent an inquiry to Most, whose reply proved a warning against me. Unknown to me, Bauer shared the room I occupied in Nold&#39;s house. Through the long hours of the night he lay awake, with revolver cocked. At the first sign of a suspicious move on my part, he had determined to kill me.

The personal tenor of our correspondence is gradually broadening into the larger scope of socio-political theories, methods of agitation, and applied tactics. The discussions, prolonged and often heated, absorb our interest. The bulky notes necessitate greater circumspection; the difficulty of procuring writing materials assumes a serious aspect. Every available scrap of paper is exhausted; margins of stray newspapers and magazines have been penciled on, the contents repeatedly erased, and the frayed tatters microscopically covered with ink. Even an occasional fly-leaf from library books has been sacrilegiously forced to leave its covers, and every evidence of its previous association dexterously removed. The problem threatens to terminate our correspondence and fills us with dismay. But the genius our faithful postman, of proud horsethieving proclivities, proves equal to the occasion: Bob constitutes himself our commissary, designating the broom shop, in which he is employed, as the base of our future supplies.

The unexpected affluence fills us with joy. The big rolls requisitioned by &quot;Horsethief&quot; exclude the fear of famine; the smooth yellow wrapping paper affords the luxury of larger and more legible chirography. The pride of sudden wealth germinates ambitious projects. We speculate on the possibility of converting our correspondence into a magazinelet, and wax warm over the proposed list of readers. Before long the first issue of the <em>Zuchthausbluethen</em><sup><a href="#fn-38" id="ref-38" aria-label="Footnote 38">[38]</a></sup> is greeted with the encouraging approval of our sole subscriber, whose contribution surprises us in the form of a rather creditable poem on the blank last page of the publication. Elated at the happy acquisition, we unanimously crown him <em>Meistersinger</em>, with dominion over the department of poetry. Soon we plan more pretentious issues: the outward size of the publication is to remain the same, three by five inches, but the number of pages is to be enlarged; each issue to have a different editor, to ensure equality of opportunity; the readers to serve as contributing editors. The appearance of the <em>Bluethen</em> is to be regulated by the time required to complete the circle of readers, whose identity is to be masked with certain initials, to protect them against discovery. Henceforth Bauer, physically a giant, is to be known as &quot;G&quot;; because of my medium stature, I shall be designated with the letter &quot;M&quot;; and Nold, as the smallest, by &quot;K.&quot;<sup><a href="#fn-39" id="ref-39" aria-label="Footnote 39">[39]</a></sup> The poet, his history somewhat shrouded in mystery, is christened &quot;D&quot; for <em>Dichter</em>. &quot;M,&quot; &quot;K,&quot; &quot;G,&quot; are to act, in turn, as editor-in-chief, whose province it is to start the <em>Bluethen</em> on its way, each reader contributing to the issue till it is returned to the original editor, to enable him to read and comment upon his fellow contributors. The publication, its contents growing transit, is finally to reach the second contributor, upon whom will devolve the editorial management of the following issue.

The unique arrangement proves a source of much pleasure and recreation. The little magazine is rich in contents and varied in style. The diversity of handwriting heightens the interest, and stimulates speculation on the personality of our increasing readers-contributors. In the arena of the diminutive publication, there rages the conflict of contending social philosophies; here a political essay rubs elbows with a witty anecdote, and a dissertation on &quot;The Nature of Things&quot; is interspersed with prison small-talk and personal reminiscence. Flashes of unstudied humor and unconscious rivalry of orthography lend peculiar charm to the unconventional editorials, and waft a breath of Josh Billings into the manuscript pages.

[Illustration: Special Spring Edition of the Z. Bluethen.]

But the success of the <em>Zuchthausbluethen</em> soon discovers itself a veritable Frankenstein, which threatens the original foundation and aims of the magazinelet. The popularity of joint editorship is growing at the cost of unity and tendency; the Bard&#39;s astonishing facility at versification, coupled with his Jules Vernian imagination, causes us grave anxiety lest his untamable Pegasus traverse the limits of our paper supply. The appalling warning of the commissary that the improvident drain upon his resources is about to force him on a strike, imperatively calls a halt. We are deliberating policies of retrenchment and economy, when unexpectedly the arrival of two Homestead men suggests an auspicious solution.

III

The presence of Hugh F. Dempsey and Robert J. Beatty, prominent in the Knights of Labor organization, offers opportunity for propaganda among workers representing the more radical element of American labor. Accused of poisoning the food served to the strike-breakers in the mills, Dempsey and Beatty appear to me men of unusual type. Be they innocent or guilty, the philosophy of their methods is in harmony with revolutionary tactics. Labor can never be unjust in its demands: is it not the creator of all the wealth in the world? Every weapon may be employed to return the despoiled People into its rightful ownership. Is not the terrorizing of scabbery, and ultimately of the capitalist exploiters, an effective means of aiding the struggle? Therefore Dempsey and Beatty deserve acclaim. Morally certain of their guilt, I respect them the more for it, though I am saddened by their denial of complicity in the scheme of wholesale extermination of the scabs. The blackleg is also human, it is true, and desires to live. But one should starve rather than turn traitor to the cause of his class. Moreover, the individual--or any number of them--cannot be weighed against the interests of humanity.

<em> </em> <em> </em> *

Infinite patience weaves the threads that bring us in contact with the imprisoned labor leaders. In the ceaseless duel of vital need against stupidity and malice, caution and wit are sharpened by danger. The least indiscretion, the most trifling negligence, means discovery, disaster. But perseverance and intelligent purpose conquer: by the aid of the faithful &quot;Horsethief,&quot; communication with Dempsey and Beatty is established. With the aggressiveness of strong conviction I present to them my views, dwelling on the historic role of the <em>Attentaeter</em> and the social significance of conscious individual protest. The discussion ramifies, the interest aroused soon transcending the limits of my paper supply. Presently I am involved in a correspondence with several men, whose questions and misinterpretations regarding my act I attempt to answer and correct with individual notes. But the method proves an impossible tax on our opportunities, and &quot;KGM&quot; finally decide to publish an English edition of the <em>Zuchthausbluethen</em>. The German magazinelet is suspended, and in its place appears the first issue of the <em>Prison Blossoms</em>.

CHAPTER XIII

THE JUDAS

&quot;Ah, there, Sporty!&quot; my assistant greets me in the shop. &quot;Stand treat on this festive occasion?&quot;

&quot;Yes, Red. Have a chew,&quot; I reply with a smile, handing him my fresh plug of tobacco.

His eyes twinkle with mischievous humor as he scrutinizes my changed suit of dark gray. The larger part of the plug swelling out his cheek, he flings to me the remnant across the table, remarking:

&quot;Don&#39;t care for&#39;t. Take back your choo, I&#39;ll keep me honor,--your plug, I mean, sonny. A gentleman of my eminence, sir, a natural-born navigator on the high seas of social life,--are you on, me bye?--a gentleman, I repeat, sir, whose canoe the mutations of all that is human have chucked on this here dry, thrice damned dry latitude, sir, this nocuous plague-spot of civilization,--say, kid, what t&#39; hell am I talkin&#39; about? Damn if I ain&#39;t clean forgot.&quot;

&quot;I&#39;m sure I don&#39;t know, Red.&quot;

&quot;Like hell you don&#39;t! It&#39;s your glad duds, kid. Offerin&#39; <em>me</em> a ch-aw tob-b-bac-co! Christ, I&#39;m dyin&#39; for a drop of booze. This magnificent occasion deserves a wetting, sir. And, say, Aleck, it won&#39;t hurt your beauty to stretch them sleeves of yours a bit. You look like a scarecrow in them high-water pants. Ain&#39;t old Sandy the king of skinners, though!&quot;

&quot;Whom do you mean, Red?&quot;

&quot;Who I mean, you idjot! Who but that skunk of a Warden, the Honorable Captain Edward S. Wright, if you please, sir. Captain of rotten old punks, that&#39;s what he is. You ask th&#39; screws. He&#39;s never smelt powder; why, he&#39;s been <em>here</em> most o&#39; his life. But some o&#39; th&#39; screws been here longer, borned here, damn &#39;em; couldn&#39;t pull &#39;em out o&#39; here with a steam engine, you couldn&#39;t. They can tell you all &#39;bout the Cap, though. Old Sandy didn&#39; have a plugged nickel to his name when he come &#39;ere, an&#39; now the damn stomach-robber is rich. Reg&#39;lar gold mine this dump&#39;s for &#39;im. Only gets a lousy five thousan&#39; per year. Got big fam&#39;ly an&#39; keeps carriages an&#39; servants, see, an&#39; can &#39;ford t&#39; go to Europe every year, an&#39; got a big pile in th&#39; bank to boot, all on a scurvy five thousan&#39; a year. Good manager, ain&#39;t he? A reg&#39;lar church member, too, damn his rotten soul to hell!&quot;

&quot;Is he as bad as all that, Red?&quot;

&quot;Is he? A hypocrite dyed in th&#39; wool, that&#39;s what he is. Plays the humanitarian racket. He had a great deal t&#39; say t&#39; the papers why he didn&#39;t believe in the brutal way Iams was punished by that Homestead colonel--er--what&#39;s &#39;is name?&quot;

&quot;Colonel Streator, of the Tenth Pennsylvania.&quot;

&quot;That&#39;s the cur. He hung up Private Iams by the thumbs till th&#39; poor boy was almost dead. For nothin&#39;, too. Suppose you remember, don&#39;t you? Iams had called for &#39;three cheers for the man who shot Frick,&#39; an&#39; they pretty near killed &#39;im for &#39;t, an&#39; then drummed &#39;im out of th&#39; regiment with &#39;is head half shaved.&quot;

&quot;It was a most barbarous thing.&quot;

&quot;An&#39; that damn Sandy swore in th&#39; papers he didn&#39;t believe in such things, an&#39; all th&#39; while th&#39; lyin&#39; murderer is doin&#39; it himself. Not a day but some poor con is &#39;cuffed up&#39; in th&#39; hole. That&#39;s th&#39; kind of humanitarian <em>he</em> is! It makes me wild t&#39; think on &#39;t. Why, kid, I even get a bit excited, and forget that you, young sir, are attuned to the dulcet symphonies of classic English. But whenever that skunk of a Warden is the subject of conversation, sir, even my usually imperturbable serenity of spirit and tranquil stoicism are not equal to &#39;Patience on a monument smiling at grief.&#39; Watch me, sonny, that&#39;s yours truly spielin&#39;. Why, look at them dingy rags of yours. I liked you better in th&#39; striped duds. They give you the hand-me-downs of that nigger that went out yesterday, an&#39; charge you on th&#39; books with a bran&#39; new suit. See where Sandy gets his slice, eh? An&#39; say, kid, how long are you here?&quot;

&quot;About eight months, Red.&quot;

&quot;They beat you out o&#39; two months all right. Suppose they obey their own rules? Nit, sir. You are aware, my precious lamb, that you are entitled to discard your polychromic vestments of zebra hue after a sojourn of six months in this benevolent dump. I bet you that fresh fish at the loopin&#39; machine there, came up &#39;ere some days ago, <em>he</em> won&#39;t be kept waitin&#39; more&#39;n six months for &#39;is black clothes.&quot;

I glance in the direction of the recent arrival. He is a slender man, with swarthy complexion and quick, shifting eye. The expression of guilty cunning is repelling.

&quot;Who is that man?&quot; I whisper to the assistant.

&quot;Like &#39;im, don&#39;t you? Permit me, sir, to introduce to you the handiwork of his Maker, a mealy-mouthed, oily-lipped, scurvy gaycat, a yellow cur, a snivelling, fawning stool, a filthy, oozy sneak, a snake in the grass whose very presence, sir, is a mortal insult to a self-respecting member of my clan,--Mr. Patrick Gallagher, of the honorable Pinkerton family, sir.&quot;

&quot;Gallagher?&quot; I ask, in astonishment. &quot;The informer, who denounced Dempsey and Beatty?&quot;

&quot;The very same. The dirty snitch that got those fellows railroaded here for seven years. Dempsey was a fool to bunch up with such vermin as Gallagher and Davidson. He was Master Workman of some district of the Knights of Labor. Why in hell didn&#39;t he get his own men to do th&#39; job? Goes to work an&#39; hires a brace of gaycats; sent &#39;em to the scab mills, you savvy, to sling hash for the blacklegs and keep &#39;im posted on the goings on, see? S&#39;pose you have oriented yourself, sir, concerning the developments in the culinary experiment?&quot;

&quot;Yes. Croton oil is supposed to have been used to make the scabs sick with diarrhoea.&quot;

&quot;Make &#39;em sick? Why, me bye, scores of &#39;em croaked. I am surprised, sir, at your use of such a vulgar term as diarrhoea. You offend my aestheticism. The learned gentlemen who delve deeply into the bowels of earth and man, sir, ascribed the sudden and phenomenal increase of unmentionable human obligations to nature, the mysterious and extravagant popularity of the houses of ill odor, sir, and the automatic obedience to their call, as due entirely to the dumping of a lot o&#39; lousy bums, sir, into filthy quarters, or to impurities of the liquid supply, or to--pardon my frankness, sir--to intestinal effeminacy, which, in flaccid excitability, persisted in ill-timed relaxation unseemly in well-mannered Christians. Some future day, sir, there may arise a poet to glorify with beauteous epic the heroic days of the modern Bull Run--an&#39; I kin tell you, laddie, they run and kept runnin&#39;, top and bottom--or some lyric bard may put to Hudibrastic verse--watch me climbin&#39; th&#39; Parnassus, kid--the poetic feet, the numbers, the assonance, and strain of the inspiring days when Croton Oil was King. Yes, sirree; but for yours truly, me hand ain&#39;t in such pies; and moreover, sir, I make it an invariable rule of gentlemanly behavior t&#39; keep me snout out o&#39; other people&#39;s biz.&quot;

&quot;Dempsey may be innocent, Red.&quot;

&quot;Well, th&#39; joory didn&#39;t think so. But there&#39;s no tellin&#39;. Honest t&#39; God, Aleck, that rotten scab of a Gallagher has cast the pale hue of resolution, if I may borrow old Billy Shake&#39;s slang, sir, over me gener&#39;ly settled convictions. You know, in the abundant plenitude of my heterogeneous experience with all sorts and conditions of rats and gaycats, sir, fortified by a natural genius of no mean order, of 1859 vintage, damme if I ever run across such an acute form of confessionitis as manifested by the lout on th&#39; loopin&#39; machine there. You know what he done yesterday?&quot;

&quot;What?&quot;

&quot;Sent for th&#39; distric&#39; attorney and made another confesh.&quot;

&quot;Really? How do you know?&quot;

&quot;Night screw&#39;s a particular fren&#39; o&#39; mine, kid. I shtands in, see? The mick&#39;s a reg&#39;lar Yahoo, can&#39;t hardly spell &#39;is own name. He daily requisitions upon my humble but abundant intelligence, sir, to make out his reports. Catch on, eh? I&#39;ve never earned a hand-out with more dignified probity, sir. It&#39;s a cinch. Last night he gimme a great slice of corn dodger. It was A 1, I tell you, an&#39; two hard boiled eggs and half a tomato, juicy and luscious, sir. Didn&#39;t I enjoy it, though! Makes your mouth water, eh, kid? Well, you be good t&#39; me, an&#39; you kin have what I got. I&#39;ll divvy up with you. We-ll! Don&#39; stand there an&#39; gape at me like a wooden Injun. Has the unexpected revelation of my magnanimous generosity deprived you of articulate utterance, sir?&quot;
